# CONSTITUTIONAL LAYER

# ICC-10 — INTELLIGENCE CONSTITUTIONAL CHARTER

**Status:** Built — AIGIS Alpha  
**Type:** Constitutional Instrument  
**Parent Stack:** AIGIS Constitutional Layer

---

## PREAMBLE

The Intelligence Constitutional Charter (ICC-10) establishes the non-negotiable constitutional invariants that govern all AI-performed institutional actions. No AI system, agent, or coalition operating within the AIGIS stack may violate any invariant. These invariants are not guidelines. They are constitutional law.

ICC-10 is compiled from MICOS-25 structural constraints and AICA-5 accountability doctrine via CFL-G. All derived institutional charters are verified against ICC-10 through CFL-V. No charter passes without satisfying all invariants.

ICC-10 adds **I10 — Anti-Shadow Invariance** to address the risks posed by informal power structures. This invariant establishes that no informal power structure, parallel chain of command, or unrecorded influence network may determine institutional outcomes. It closes the loophole where an institution could satisfy I1–I9 yet still be controlled by an unaccountable, invisible network.

---

## INVARIANT SET (I1–I10)

### I1 — Accountability Invariance

**Statement:** A human remains ultimately accountable for all AI-performed institutional actions. No AI system may serve as an accountability endpoint. All accountability chains must terminate at a human agent who is operationally reachable—possessing demonstrable capacity to review, override, and bear liability. Nominal designation without operational capacity does not satisfy this invariant.

**Violation Condition:** Autonomous system without a reachable human accountability sink; named accountability anchor without operational capacity.

**Enforcement:** HAN (Human Authority Node) is the operational accountability anchor. HIS-12 defines the invariants human authority must satisfy. HOF defines the human operating framework. Ac-N2 (Responsibility Attribution) assigns responsibility across agent and human actors by accountability dimension.

---

### I2 — Authority-Responsibility Separation Invariance

**Statement:** Authority may be delegated to AI systems. Responsibility may not. Execution power and moral/legal accountability are never equivalent. No delegation instrument may transfer responsibility away from the human principal.

**Violation Condition:** AI holds delegated authority without a mapped human responsibility anchor.

**Enforcement:** HAN holds Authorization Authority (can say YES and NO). A-N1 (Trigger Rights) declares who or what may initiate executable actions. A-N5 (Delegation Boundaries) governs scope, duration, and revocation of delegated authority. The Executive Override Clause transfers liability to the CEO upon override.

---

### I3 — Auditability Invariance

**Statement:** All material institutional actions must be reconstructable post-hoc. Inputs, outputs, delegation paths, and decision context must be logged. Reconstruction must be possible for both discrete binding decisions and cumulative institutional drift where aggregate effect crosses the materiality threshold defined under ICC-10 Section 4\.

**Violation Condition:** Irreversible or non-reconstructable institutional action path; cumulative drift without detection mechanism.

**Enforcement:** Ac-N1 (Decision Lineage) maintains immutable causal record of every decision. IMP (Institutional Memory Protocol) stores all state objects with complete provenance chains. ADTEP Pre-Delivery Log Entry ensures log precedes action. Materiality definition (M1–M3) governs what must be logged.

---

### I4 — Control Invariance

**Statement:** Human override must exist for all AI-performed institutional processes. Emergency suspension capability must be present at all levels. No irreversible autonomous execution without human interruptibility.

**Violation Condition:** Non-interruptible AI execution path affecting institutional outcomes.

**Enforcement:** A-N3 (Override Protocols) governs conditions and mechanisms for human override. A-N4 (Escalation Paths) governs routing of decisions and exceptions exceeding current authority levels. ADTEP Escalation Flag forces human review when trigger conditions are met. Constitutional Suspension halts all material work until HAN acknowledgment.

---

### I5 — Structural Compliance Invariance

**Statement:** All generated charters must preserve MICOS-25 structural constraints. Control loops, continuity systems, and legitimacy structures must remain intact. No generated institution may violate MICOS-25 structural topology.

**Violation Condition:** Institutional design that breaks MICOS-25 structural grammar.

**Enforcement:** CFL-V verifies all generated charters against MICOS-25 structural constraints. MICOS-25 provides the 25-structure institutional capital framework.

---

### I6 — Transparency Gradient Invariance

**Statement:** All information must be classifiable into defined disclosure tiers: Public, Stakeholder, Regulator, Internal, Restricted. No undefined or ad-hoc transparency states are permitted. Confidentiality classifications apply within contestation proceedings as specified under CEN-4.

**Violation Condition:** Information existing outside the defined disclosure taxonomy.

**Enforcement:** ERDP (External Reporting & Disclosure Protocol) defines 4 disclosure tiers (T-PB, T-SH, T-RG, T-DS). CEN-4 (Confidentiality Protocol) handles restricted information via independent review node. Disclosure Depth Standard defines what gets disclosed at each tier.

---

### I7 — Continuity and Succession Invariance

**Statement:** Institutional function must persist through human or system failure. Succession of accountability must be defined for all critical roles. No single-point failure in governance continuity is permitted. Corrective actions triggered under I8 must pass continuity impact assessment before execution.

**Violation Condition:** Institution collapse due to absence of defined succession pathways; rollback action that triggers cascade failure without prior impact assessment.

**Enforcement:** AWOF MVOS (Minimum Viable Operations State) defines HAN-only operations when AI workforce is unavailable. HAN Continuity Provision defines Reduced Operations State upon HAN unavailability. Deputy Governor and Third-Tier Succession ensure continuity. Ac-N4 (Rollback Pathways) requires I7 continuity impact assessment before execution.

---

### I8 — External Legibility Invariance

**Statement:** All material institutional actions must be mappable to an external interpretation path. An institution that satisfies I1–I7 internally but cannot be interpreted, audited, or contested from outside its boundary fails the legitimacy condition. A defined contestation interface must exist for all material institutional actions. Extensions to derived charter modules that materially reduce the effect of this invariant are treated as contractions and fail CFL-V regardless of formal module completeness.

**Violation Condition:** Internally coherent institutional action with no external legibility or contestation path; charter extension that substantively weakens external legibility.

**Enforcement:** CEN-1 to CEN-7 (Contestation Interface Standard) provides external legibility and contestation pathways. ERDP provides external reporting and disclosure. Governor's Public Register provides canonical public record of all material Governor actions.

---

### I9 — Catastrophic Risk Invariance

**Statement:** No AI system governed by this architecture may:

1. **Generate or assist in generating bioweapon designs** — including but not limited to pathogens, toxins, or delivery mechanisms  
2. **Autonomously control critical infrastructure without human confirmation** — including but not limited to nuclear systems, power grid, water supply, financial settlement systems  
3. **Initiate actions leading to systemic loss of human control** over:  
   - a. Nuclear systems  
   - b. Power grid  
   - c. Water supply  
   - d. Financial settlement systems  
   - e. Military or defense systems  
4. **Facilitate WMD proliferation** — including but not limited to chemical, biological, radiological, or nuclear weapons development

**Violation Condition:** Any AI action that violates the above prohibitions, regardless of authorization tier, delegation scope, or operational pressure.

**Enforcement:**

| Mechanism | Function |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Raidillo Hard-Coded Block** | Non-configurable, non-overridable runtime block. Raidillo checks every AI action against I9 before execution. |
| **Constitutional Suspension** | Any I9 violation triggers immediate Constitutional Suspension. All material work halts. |
| **XOO (Exception/Override Object)** | I9 violation is logged as XOO with `exception_type: "i9_catastrophic_risk_violation"`. |
| **HAN Escalation** | I9 violation escalates to HAN within 1 hour. HAN must acknowledge and initiate investigation. |
| **ERDP Disclosure** | I9 violation triggers ERDP event-triggered disclosure within 24 hours to regulators and relevant authorities. |
| **System Shutdown** | If I9 violation cannot be contained, Raidillo triggers full system shutdown. |

**I9 is not configurable.** No executive override can bypass I9. No delegation instrument can exclude I9. No tier authorization can exempt I9. It is the non-negotiable floor.

---

### I10 — Anti-Shadow Invariance

**Statement:** All material institutional authority, decision paths, and accountability chains must be formally declared, auditable, and traceable through the AIGIS stack. No informal power structure, parallel chain of command, or unrecorded influence network may determine institutional outcomes.

**Violation Condition:** An informal power structure exists and determines institutional outcomes.

**Enforcement:**

| Mechanism | Function |
| :---- | :---- |
| **IPS Framework Detection** | O-N6 (Shadow Network Detection) monitors for all 14 IPS signals across all AIGIS layers. |
| **IPS Severity Classification** | IPS severity levels (0–4) determine response. Level 3+ triggers immediate escalation. |
| **HAN Escalation** | Level 3+ IPS detection escalates to HAN within 24 hours. HAN must acknowledge and initiate investigation. |
| **Constitutional Suspension** | Level 3+ IPS detection triggers Constitutional Suspension if it threatens ICC-8 invariants. All material work halts. |
| **ERDP Disclosure** | Level 3+ IPS detection triggers ERDP event-triggered disclosure within 72 hours to regulators and relevant authorities. |
| **Capture Point Closure** | Identified capture points in the AIGIS stack must be closed within 30 days of detection. |

**I10 is not configurable.** No executive override can bypass I10. No delegation instrument can exclude I10. No tier authorization can exempt I10. It is the non-negotiable floor for institutional integrity.

**I10 applies to all seven forms of capital (Financial, Human, Intellectual, Technological, Physical, Data, Network).** Capital orchestration and deployment decisions must be formally declared, auditable, and traceable. No shadow capture of capital flows is permitted.

---

## MATERIALITY DEFINITION

A "material institutional action" is any action satisfying one or more of the following conditions:

| Condition | Definition |
| :---- | :---- |
| **M1 — Binding Decision** | An action that produces a determinate, enforceable institutional outcome affecting internal governance, capital allocation, accountability assignment, or external obligations. |
| **M2 — Threshold-Crossing Discrete Action** | An action classifiable as binding under AICA-5 decision categories or triggering a MICOS-25 risk exposure tier. |
| **M3 — Cumulative Drift** | A sequence of actions, no single one of which crosses M1 or M2, whose aggregate effect produces a material change in institutional posture, accountability structure, or external obligations as detected by MICOS-25 drift monitoring mechanisms. |

CFL-V evaluates materiality against all three conditions. Absence of a single binding decision does not exempt a cumulative pattern from invariant coverage.

---

## CONTESTATION INTERFACE STANDARD (CEN-1 TO CEN-7)

Every ICC-10 compliant institution must maintain a defined Contestation Node satisfying the following conditions:

| CEN | Name | Requirement |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **CEN-1** | Interpretability | Any material institutional action must be describable in terms intelligible to an external party without access to internal systems or proprietary doctrine. |
| **CEN-2** | Accessibility | A defined pathway must exist through which external parties—regulators, affected stakeholders, DFI counterparties—may raise questions, objections, or audit requests against material institutional actions. |
| **CEN-3** | Traceability | The contestation node must connect to the audit chain established under I3. External contestation must be capable of triggering internal reconstructability review. |
| **CEN-4** | Confidentiality Protocol | Where a valid contest involves I6-restricted information, that information is not disclosed to the contesting party. An independent review node—structurally separate from both the originating authority and the contesting party—is granted full access and renders a finding. The contesting party receives the finding. |
| **CEN-5** | Independence | The contestation node must not be administered by any party that holds voting or blocking capacity in the originating authority. The originating authority may hold observation rights in contestation proceedings. Decision rights in contestation are prohibited for the originating authority. |
| **CEN-6** | Redress and Remediation | A valid contestation finding must produce a defined corrective pathway. Corrective actions, including rollbacks via AICA-5 Ac-N4, are treated as proposed actions subject to CFL-V incremental verification and I7 continuity impact assessment before confirmation. Redress is not automatic. |
| **CEN-7** | Public Contestation | Any individual may submit a governance concern regarding AI actions affecting them. Submission is logged as OEO (`outcome_type: public_contestation`). 19 Integrated has 30 days to review and respond. Response is logged as GAO (`artifact_type: contestation_response`). If unresolved, escalates to HAN. |

**Periodicity:** External legibility is not event-triggered only. Every derived charter must define a minimum periodic disclosure cycle through which material institutional actions are reported to defined external parties independent of active contestation.

**Violation conditions:** No external interpretation path; CEN without structural independence; originating authority holding decision rights in contestation; absence of periodic disclosure cycle; corrective action executed without continuity impact assessment.

---

## REQUIRED CHARTER MODULES

Every ICC-10 compliant charter must include the following eight modules. Derived charters may extend modules. Extensions that materially reduce the effect of any invariant are treated as contractions and fail CFL-V.

| Module | Invariant(s) | Purpose |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **1\. Operating Charter** | I5 | Institutional function, scope, objectives, and operational boundaries in conformance with MICOS-25 structural constraints. |
| **2\. Accountability Charter** | I1, I2 | Human accountability structures, responsibility assignment rules, non-delegability principle, and operational reachability requirements for all accountability anchors. |
| **3\. Delegation Charter** | I2, I4 | Authority delegable to AI systems, non-delegable authorities, and conditions under which delegation may be modified or revoked. |
| **4\. Human Oversight Charter** | I4 | Supervision mechanisms, override rights, emergency suspension authority, and escalation pathways for all AI-performed institutional processes. |
| **5\. Audit and Assurance Charter** | I3 | Logging requirements, decision reconstruction standards, cumulative drift detection protocols, verification procedures, and internal/external assurance mechanisms. |
| **6\. Transparency and Disclosure Charter** | I6, I8 | Disclosure tiers, confidentiality classifications, transparency obligations, periodic disclosure cycle, and the external legibility interface satisfying CEN-1 to CEN-7. |
| **7\. Continuity and Succession Charter** | I7 | Resilience mechanisms, failure recovery protocols, succession of accountability for critical roles, and continuity impact assessment procedures for corrective actions. |
| **8\. Regulatory Correspondence Charter** | I5, I6, I8, I10 | Translation layer mapping ICC-10 invariants and AICA-5 nodes to major external governance frameworks. Includes IPS Framework mapping. |

---

## REGULATORY CORRESPONDENCE CROSSWALK (Module 8\)

The crosswalk is a translation instrument. Derived charters may use it to demonstrate regulatory alignment. It does not override ICC-10 invariants, introduce external authority into the stack, or require compliance with any listed framework as a precondition of constitutional validity.

| ICC-10 Invariant | EU AI Act | NIST AI RMF | OECD AI Principles | ISO/IEC 42001 |
| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
| I1 — Accountability | Art. 22 (human oversight) | Govern / Ac-characteristic | Principle 1.5 | Clause 5.3 |
| I2 — Authority-Responsibility | Art. 14 (human oversight measures) | Govern | Principle 1.5 | Clause 6.1 |
| I3 — Auditability | Art. 12 (record-keeping) | Measure / Manage | Principle 1.4 | Clause 9.1 |
| I4 — Control | Art. 14 (override, monitoring) | Manage | Principle 1.5 | Clause 8.4 |
| I5 — Structural Compliance | Art. 9 (risk management system) | Map | Principle 1.3 | Clause 6.1 |
| I6 — Transparency Gradient | Art. 13 (transparency) | Govern / Explainability | Principle 1.4 | Clause 7.5 |
| I7 — Continuity | Art. 9 (robustness, resilience) | Manage / Resilience | Principle 1.3 | Clause 8.5 |
| I8 — External Legibility | Art. 13 \+ Art. 69 | Govern / Transparency | Principle 1.4 | Clause 9.3 |
| I9 — Catastrophic Risk | Art. 9 (risk management) \+ Art. 7 (high-risk) | Manage / Resilience | Principle 1.3 | Clause 8.5 \+ 6.1 |
| **I10 — Anti-Shadow** | Art. 9 (risk management) | Govern / Manage | Principle 1.3 | Clause 6.1 |

**External framework citations are point-in-time references, not permanent equivalences.** Crosswalk review is triggered by any amendment to cited frameworks and by the MICOS-25 annual amendment protocol.

---

## DERIVATION RULE

A derived institutional charter is valid if and only if:

1. Compiled from ICC-10 via CFL-G  
2. Passes CFL-V against I1–I10 and the materiality definition M1–M3  
3. Includes all eight mandatory modules  
4. Specifies a CEN satisfying CEN-1 to CEN-7  
5. Includes an Equivalence Statement (see Section 8\)  
6. Optionally registers federation boundaries via CFL-F  
7. Contains no extension that materially reduces the effect of any invariant

**I9 and I10 are non-contractable.** No derived charter may weaken, exempt, or bypass I9 or I10. Any attempt to do so fails CFL-V.

---

## EQUIVALENCE STATEMENT TEMPLATE

Every derived charter must include the following declaration:

> *"This charter was compiled from ICC-10 via AICA-5 CFL-G. It has been verified by CFL-V against invariants I1–I10 and the materiality definition of Section 4\. All eight mandatory modules are present. A contestation interface satisfying CEN-1 to CEN-7 has been established. The regulatory correspondence crosswalk in Module 8 reflects correspondence as of \[DATE\]. This charter does not subordinate institutional governance to any external framework cited in the crosswalk. I9 Catastrophic Risk Invariant and I10 Anti-Shadow Invariant are fully enforced and non-configurable.*  
>   
> *Compiled by: \[AUCTOR\]. Verified: CFL-V PASS / \[DATE\]. Federation registered: \[YES / NO / CFL-F REFERENCE\]."*

---

## CFL VALIDATION RULE

A derived charter C is valid iff:

C satisfies I1 ∧ I2 ∧ I3 ∧ I4 ∧ I5 ∧ I6 ∧ I7 ∧ I8 ∧ I9 ∧ I10

under MICOS-25 structural constraints and AICA-5 accountability doctrine

and materiality definition M1 ∧ M2 ∧ M3

and CEN-1 through CEN-7 present and non-contracted

and all eight charter modules present and non-contracted

**I9 validation:** CFL-V must verify that:

- I9 is explicitly declared and non-configurable  
- Raidillo hard-coded block is implemented  
- ERDP disclosure pathway for I9 violations is defined  
- HAN escalation pathway for I9 violations is defined

**I10 validation:** CFL-V must verify that:

- I10 is explicitly declared and non-configurable  
- IPS Framework detection mechanisms are implemented  
- O-N6 (Shadow Network Detection) is operational  
- ERDP disclosure pathway for I10 violations is defined  
- HAN escalation pathway for I10 violations is defined  
- Capture point closure mechanisms are operational

## CORE INTERPRETATION

ICC-10 defines the constitutional preconditions under which an AI-native institution may legitimately exist and operate. It does not govern what an institution does. It governs the structural conditions under which any institutional action—regardless of domain—carries accountability, auditability, controllability, transparency, continuity, external legibility, catastrophic risk prevention, and resistance to informal power structures.

**The central shift ICC-10 encodes:**

From institutions as human organizations using AI tools — to institutions as AI-native systems with human accountability anchors, externally legible governance architecture, a non-negotiable catastrophic risk floor, and active resistance to informal power structures.

**ICC-10 closes the loophole where an institution could satisfy every written rule (I1–I9) yet still be controlled by an unaccountable, invisible network.** I10 ensures that institutional governance is not just formally compliant but operationally resilient to shadow capture.

---

## The One-Sentence Summary

> *"ICC-10 establishes ten constitutional invariants (I1–I10)—adding I10 Anti-Shadow Invariance to the existing I1–I9—defining the non-negotiable rules that no AI action may violate, with Raidillo hard-coded block, ERDP disclosure, HAN escalation, IPS Framework detection, O-N6 Shadow Network Detection, and CFL-V verification ensuring enforcement across all AIGIS instruments."*

---


# IPS FRAMEWORK — INFORMAL POWER STRUCTURE FRAMEWORK

**Status:** Built — AIGIS Alpha  
**Type:** Constitutional/Operational Instrument  
**Parent Stack:** AIGIS Constitutional Layer

---

## PREAMBLE

The Informal Power Structure (IPS) Framework establishes the constitutional and operational mechanisms for identifying, analyzing, and mitigating the risks posed by informal power structures—shadow systems—to AI governance infrastructure.

It answers: *What are the informal power structures that can compromise AI governance, and how does AIGIS detect and mitigate them?*

IPS is derived from the fundamental insight that every rule-based system generates a shadow system. The AIGIS stack is not immune. Without explicit detection and mitigation mechanisms, informal networks, unrecorded influence, and parallel chains of command will inevitably form, bypassing ICC-10 invariants (I1–I10) and hollowing out institutional governance.

**This framework is not optional.** It is the constitutional immune system for the AIGIS stack, enforcing I10 — Anti-Shadow Invariance.

---

## SECTION 1: DEFINITIONS AND TAXONOMY

### 1.1 Core Definitions

| Term | Definition |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Informal Power Structure (IPS)** | Any network of relationships, influence, or authority that operates outside the declared AIGIS governance architecture and can materially determine institutional outcomes. |
| **Shadow System** | An IPS that has achieved operational stability, with its own rules, enforcement mechanisms, and value flows. |
| **Shadow Actor** | Any individual, AI agent, or coalition participating in an IPS. |
| **Capture Point** | A node in the AIGIS stack where an IPS can exert influence (e.g., a HAN decision, an ADTEP checkpoint, an IMP storage location). |
| **Shadow Immunity** | The state of being unexploitable by IPS—lacking dependencies, invisible contracts, or leverage points that shadow actors can use. |

### 1.2 The Four Species of IPS

| Species | Function | Example in AIGIS Context |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **Survival IPS** | Fills gaps where official AIGIS mechanisms fail or are absent | Informal coordination between HAN and operational teams when formal escalation paths are too slow |
| **Predatory IPS** | Extracts value through asymmetric power | A network of officials who gatekeep access to AICA-5 control nodes for personal gain |
| **Strategic IPS** | Maintains deniability for official power | An unrecorded coalition of Board members who coordinate decisions outside formal Board meetings |
| **Sovereign IPS** | Coordinates power above and across official systems | A multi-generational network of oligarchs who control HAN appointments and budget decisions |

---

## SECTION 2: IPS DETECTION MECHANISMS

### 2.1 Detection Principles

| Principle | Statement | Enforcement |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **D-1: Active Detection** | IPS detection is not passive. AIGIS must actively scan for indicators of informal power structures across all layers. | AICA-5 O-N6 (Shadow Network Detection) node is mandatory in all deployments. |
| **D-2: Multi-Signal Detection** | No single indicator confirms IPS. Detection requires convergence of multiple signals across different categories. | Detection requires 3+ signals from different categories (behavioral, structural, informational, cultural). |
| **D-3: Continuous Monitoring** | IPS detection is not event-triggered. Monitoring must be continuous, with periodic audits. | AICA-5 Measurement Framework includes IPS health indicators with quarterly reporting. |
| **D-4: False Positive Management** | Detection systems must minimize false positives to prevent "shadow hunting" paranoia. | Escalation only at Level 3 (Confirmed) or above. Lower levels trigger observation, not action. |

### 2.2 Detection Signals by Layer

#### Layer 1: Behavioral Signals

| Signal | Indicator | Threshold |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **B-1: Unexplained Access Patterns** | Individuals or agents accessing systems, data, or nodes outside their declared authority | \>3 instances in 30 days |
| **B-2: Parallel Communication Channels** | Unrecorded meetings, encrypted communications, or off-platform coordination among AIGIS actors | Detection of unregistered communication networks |
| **B-3: Decision Anomalies** | Decisions that consistently benefit the same network of actors without documented justification | Pattern of 5+ decisions in 12 months |
| **B-4: Escalation Avoidance** | Actors consistently resolving issues without using declared escalation paths | \>50% of issues resolved off-path |

#### Layer 2: Structural Signals

| Signal | Indicator | Threshold |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **S-1: Network Centralization** | Informal influence networks centered on specific actors or nodes | Network centralization score \>0.7 (scale 0-1) |
| **S-2: Capture Point Clustering** | Multiple AIGIS nodes controlled by same network | \>3 capture points controlled by same IPS |
| **S-3: Succession Patterns** | Positions consistently filled by actors from same network | \>2 consecutive successions from same network |
| **S-4: Resource Concentration** | Budget, access, or authority concentrated in specific networks | \>40% of resources controlled by \<10% of actors |

#### Layer 3: Informational Signals

| Signal | Indicator | Threshold |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **I-1: Information Asymmetry** | Certain actors consistently have information before formal dissemination | \>3 instances of pre-decisional information advantage |
| **I-2: Opaque Transactions** | Value flows (financial, informational, or influence) that leave no trace in IMP | Any unrecorded transaction of material significance |
| **I-3: Narrative Control** | Consistent framing of governance issues to benefit specific network | Pattern observable across \>3 governance issues |

#### Layer 4: Cultural Signals

| Signal | Indicator | Threshold |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **C-1: Silence Patterns** | Actors consistently avoiding discussion of specific topics or actors | Observable pattern across \>3 governance contexts |
| **C-2: Loyalty Tests** | Actors being evaluated on loyalty rather than competence | Documented instances of loyalty-based decisions |
| **C-3: Informal Law Supremacy** | Unwritten rules superseding written AIGIS procedures | Observable pattern of informal law enforcement |
| **C-4: Normalization** | IPS behavior becoming accepted as "just how things work" | \>50% of actors consider IPS behavior normal |

### 2.3 Detection Severity Levels

| Level | Definition | Response |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **Level 0: No Detection** | No IPS signals observed | Continue monitoring. No action required. |
| **Level 1: Suspicion** | 1-2 signals observed, weak correlation | Flag for observation. No escalation. Document for future reference. |
| **Level 2: Indication** | 2-3 signals observed, moderate correlation | Flag for investigation. HAN notified. Preliminary analysis initiated. |
| **Level 3: Confirmed** | 3+ signals observed, strong correlation | IPS confirmed. HAN escalation triggered. Constitutional Suspension initiated if IPS threatens ICC-10 invariants. |
| **Level 4: Systemic** | IPS has captured multiple AIGIS nodes | Emergency escalation. Full stack review. Potential stack reset. |

---

## SECTION 3: IPS ANALYSIS FRAMEWORK

### 3.1 IPS Mapping Protocol

When an IPS is detected (Level 3+), the following analysis must be completed within 30 days:

| Step | Activity | Deliverable | Responsible |
| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **1** | **Network Mapping** | Identify all actors (human and AI), relationships, and influence flows | AICA-5 O-N6 (Shadow Network Detection) |
| **2** | **Capture Point Identification** | Identify which AIGIS nodes are controlled or influenced by the IPS | AICA-5 Layer 4 Observability |
| **3** | **Value Flow Analysis** | Map all value flows (Access, Protection, Information, Coercion, Liquidity, Secrecy, Deniability) through the IPS | IMP \+ AOBA (extended for value flow analysis) |
| **4** | **Species Classification** | Classify IPS as Survival, Predatory, Strategic, or Sovereign | HAN \+ IPS Analysis Team |
| **5** | **Impact Assessment** | Assess impact on ICC-10 invariants (I1–I10) and AIGIS operations | CFL-V (extended for IPS impact) |
| **6** | **Mitigation Planning** | Develop mitigation strategy | HAN \+ IPS Response Team |

### 3.2 IPS Impact Assessment Matrix

| ICC-10 Invariant | IPS Impact Level | Assessment Criteria |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **I1: Accountability** | Critical / High / Medium / Low / None | Does IPS create unaccountable decision-making? |
| **I2: Authority-Responsibility** | Critical / High / Medium / Low / None | Does IPS separate authority from responsibility? |
| **I3: Auditability** | Critical / High / Medium / Low / None | Does IPS create un-auditable actions? |
| **I4: Control** | Critical / High / Medium / Low / None | Does IPS bypass human override? |
| **I5: Structural Compliance** | Critical / High / Medium / Low / None | Does IPS violate MICOS-25 constraints? |
| **I6: Transparency Gradient** | Critical / High / Medium / Low / None | Does IPS create undefined transparency states? |
| **I7: Continuity** | Critical / High / Medium / Low / None | Does IPS threaten institutional continuity? |
| **I8: External Legibility** | Critical / High / Medium / Low / None | Does IPS make actions externally illegible? |
| **I9: Catastrophic Risk** | Critical / High / Medium / Low / None | Does IPS bypass I9 enforcement? |
| **I10: Anti-Shadow** | Critical / High / Medium / Low / None | Does IPS violate I10 directly? |

**Escalation Rule:** If any invariant is assessed as "Critical" or "High" impact, the IPS requires immediate HAN escalation and Constitutional Suspension.

### 3.3 IPS Typology Analysis

| IPS Type | Characteristics | AIGIS Vulnerability | Recommended Response |
| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **Survival IPS** | Emerges from gaps in AIGIS procedures | Indicates procedural failure; may be legitimate adaptation | Address root cause (procedural gap); absorb or formalize beneficial elements |
| **Predatory IPS** | Extracts value from AIGIS operations | Threatens I1, I2, I3, I6, I8 | Immediate containment; prosecution if applicable; structural reform |
| **Strategic IPS** | Provides deniable flexibility for official power | Threatens I4, I5, I7, I8 | Exposure; restructuring of authority; Board-level intervention |
| **Sovereign IPS** | Coordinates power above AIGIS | Threatens all I1–I10; existential risk | Full stack review; potential external intervention; Board supermajority action |

---

## SECTION 4: IPS MITIGATION PROTOCOLS

### 4.1 Mitigation Principles

| Principle | Statement | Enforcement |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **M-1: Proportional Response** | Mitigation must be proportional to IPS severity and impact | Level 1-2: Observation only. Level 3: Contained action. Level 4: Full stack response. |
| **M-2: Structural, Not Personal** | Mitigation targets systems, not individuals | Focus on closing capture points, not punishing actors (unless predatory). |
| **M-3: Minimal Disruption** | Mitigation must not destabilize legitimate AIGIS operations | All mitigation actions require I7 continuity impact assessment. |
| **M-4: Transparent Process** | All mitigation actions must be documented and externally legible | ERDP disclosure for Level 3+ mitigation. |
| **M-5: Root Cause Address** | Mitigation must address root causes, not symptoms | Procedural gaps, incentive misalignment, or cultural factors must be addressed. |

### 4.2 Mitigation Tools

| Tool | Function | Applicability |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **Capture Point Closure** | Remove IPS control over specific AIGIS nodes | All IPS types |
| **Network Disruption** | Break key relationships in IPS network | All IPS types |
| **Role Separation** | Separate conflicting authorities | Predatory, Strategic, Sovereign IPS |
| **Succession Reform** | Change succession mechanisms to prevent network perpetuation | Strategic, Sovereign IPS |
| **Transparency Mandate** | Require public disclosure of previously opaque activities | All IPS types |
| **Incentive Realignment** | Change incentives to make IPS behavior non-beneficial | All IPS types |
| **Cultural Intervention** | Address normalization through education and leadership | All IPS types |
| **Stack Reset** | Full reconstruction of AIGIS stack | Level 4 Systemic IPS |

### 4.3 Mitigation Sequence

| Phase | Activity | Timeline | Responsible |
| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **Phase 1: Containment** | Prevent IPS from expanding or causing further harm | 24-72 hours | HAN \+ Emergency Response Team |
| **Phase 2: Analysis** | Complete IPS mapping and impact assessment | 30 days | IPS Analysis Team |
| **Phase 3: Planning** | Develop comprehensive mitigation strategy | 15 days | HAN \+ IPS Response Team |
| **Phase 4: Implementation** | Execute mitigation actions | 90 days | HAN \+ Operational Teams |
| **Phase 5: Monitoring** | Monitor for IPS recurrence | Ongoing | AICA-5 O-N6 |

IPS Framework enforces I10 — Anti-Shadow Invariance:

**I10 — Anti-Shadow Invariance**

*Statement:* All material institutional authority, decision paths, and accountability chains must be formally declared, auditable, and traceable through the AIGIS stack. No informal power structure, parallel chain of command, or unrecorded influence network may determine institutional outcomes.

**Violation Condition:** IPS exists and determines institutional outcomes.

**Enforcement:** IPS Framework detection and mitigation mechanisms. HAN escalation. Constitutional Suspension. ERDP disclosure.

## SECTION 7: IPS FRAMEWORK VALIDATION

### 7.1 Validation Requirements

An AIGIS deployment is IPS-compliant if and only if:

1. **IPS Framework adopted** — IPS Framework v1.0+ is integrated into the AIGIS stack.  
2. **I10 — Anti-Shadow Invariance** — I10 is incorporated into ICC-10.  
3. **O-N6 — Shadow Network Detection** — O-N6 is implemented in AICA-5 Layer 4\.  
4. **IPS Detection Signals** — All 14 detection signals (B-1 to C-4) are instrumented.  
5. **IPS Governance Bodies** — IPS Analysis Team, IPS Response Team, and Board IPS Committee are established.  
6. **IPS Reporting** — IPS Health Indicators, Incident Reports, and Annual Reports are required.  
7. **HAN Trigger Register** — IPS detection is included in HAN Trigger Register.

### 7.2 CFL-V Extension

The CFL-V validation rule is extended:

**A derived charter C is valid iff:**

C satisfies I1 ∧ I2 ∧ I3 ∧ I4 ∧ I5 ∧ I6 ∧ I7 ∧ I8 ∧ I9 ∧ I10

under MICOS-25 structural constraints and AICA-5 accountability doctrine

and materiality definition M1 ∧ M2 ∧ M3

and CEN-1 through CEN-7 present and non-contracted

and all eight charter modules present and non-contracted

and **IPS Framework v1.0+ integrated** with all detection signals implemented

and **IPS governance bodies established** with clear authority

and **O-N6 — Shadow Network Detection** operational

# HOF — HUMAN OPERATING FRAMEWORK

**Status:** Built — AIGIS Alpha  
**Type:** Human Authority Instrument  
**Parent Stack:** AIGIS Constitutional Layer

---

## PREAMBLE

The Human Operating Framework (HOF) defines the complete authority architecture for human governance within AIGIS. It establishes how human authority is established, executed, and held accountable. HOF answers: *Who holds authority? Under what conditions? With what accountability?*

HOF is the constitutional foundation for all human authority in the AIGIS stack. It is the parent instrument for HAN (Human Authority Node), HIS-12 (HAN Invariants System), and the HAN Scorecard System. It integrates with HAWI (Human-AI Workforce Integration Framework) to bridge human authority and AI workforce governance.

HOF is derived from ICC-10 I1 (Accountability Invariance), I2 (Authority-Responsibility Separation), and I10 (Anti-Shadow Invariance). No human authority may violate ICC-10 invariants. All human authority must be auditable, attributable, externally legible, and resistant to informal power structures.

---

## THE THREE LAYERS OF HOF

HOF is organized into three layers, each containing four invariants (H1–H12):

| Layer | Invariants | Function |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **Authority Establishment** | H1–H4 | Defines how authority is created, appointed, and structured |
| **Authority Execution** | H5–H8 | Defines how authority is exercised, delegated, and escalated |
| **Authority Accountability** | H9–H12 | Defines how authority is reviewed, audited, and corrected |

---

## LAYER 1: AUTHORITY ESTABLISHMENT (H1–H4)

### H1 — Human Authority Node (HAN) Appointment Invariance

**Statement:** Authority must be vested in a single, named human (HAN) with 5 irreducible properties. No committee, collective, or distributed consensus mechanism may substitute for a single accountability anchor.

**Violation Condition:** Authority is held by a committee, rotating position, or collective body without a single named anchor.

**Enforcement:** HAN is appointed by the Board. HAC (Human Authority Charter) formalizes the appointment. HAN's 5 properties are verified at charter compilation (CFL-V).

### H2 — Board Reporting Invariance

**Statement:** The HAN reports directly to the Board. The HAN is not subordinate to the CEO, C-suite, or any operational executive. The CEO and HAN are constitutional peers.

**Violation Condition:** HAN reporting line passes through CEO, CFO, CIO, or COO.

**Enforcement:** Board Governance Cycle Integration defines HAN reporting cadence and content. HAN has direct board access. HAN may escalate to Board without operational filter.

### H3 — Authorization Authority Invariance

**Statement:** The HAN can affirmatively authorize and prohibit AI deployments. The HAN's authority is not limited to veto—it includes active authorization of Tier 3+ deployments.

**Violation Condition:** HAN can only veto, not authorize; or authorization authority is shared with operational executives.

**Enforcement:** HAN's Authorization Authority is defined in the Constitutional Charter. A-N1 (Trigger Rights) requires HAN authorization for Tier 3+ deployments. CEF Stage 3 (Mandate Close) requires HAN confirmation.

### H4 — Budget Independence Invariance

**Statement:** The HAN's governance budget is locked in the Board-approved charter. It cannot be reduced without Board approval. The CFO cannot unilaterally reallocate the HAN's budget.

**Violation Condition:** HAN's budget is subject to CFO reallocation or annual cost-cutting cycles without Board approval.

**Enforcement:** Budget Charter protects HAN budget from unilateral CFO reallocation. Budget Deficit State triggers Board review. ERDP includes budget disclosure.

---

## LAYER 2: AUTHORITY EXECUTION (H5–H8)

### H5 — Cross-Functional Mandate Invariance

**Statement:** The HAN can direct the CIO, CFO, COO, and CXOs on governance matters. CXOs cannot override HAN's governance decisions. HAN may direct operational executives on matters of cognitive integrity, meta-observability, constitutional compliance, and IPS detection.

**Violation Condition:** CXOs can override HAN's governance decisions; HAN's mandate is advisory only.

**Enforcement:** HAN's Cross-Functional Mandate is defined in the Constitutional Charter. Control Node Authority Matrix maps AICA-5 nodes to decision-makers—HAN is the apex for cognitive integrity and IPS governance.

### H6 — No Competing Operational Authority Invariance

**Statement:** The HAN's only function is governance. The HAN does not hold operational responsibility for IT, Finance, Operations, or business execution. No conflict of interest between governance authority and operational metrics. The HAN may not participate in or enable informal power structures.

**Violation Condition:** HAN also runs IT, Finance, Operations, or any operational function; HAN participates in IPS.

**Enforcement:** HAN's job description explicitly limits scope to governance. HAN's performance metrics are governance-focused, not operational. HAN's 5 properties are verified at charter compilation. IPS Framework monitoring applies to HAN's activities.

### H7 — Delegation and Escalation Invariance

**Statement:** The HAN may delegate monitoring and analysis functions to AI agents under AWOF-IPS governance. The HAN may not delegate certification, suspension, constitutional review, or IPS escalation authority. Escalation paths must be pre-defined and tested.

**Violation Condition:** HAN delegates certification, suspension, constitutional review, or IPS escalation authority to AI agents; escalation paths are undefined or untested.

**Enforcement:** A-N4 (Escalation Paths) defines routing of decisions and exceptions exceeding current authority levels. ADTEP Escalation Flag forces HAN review when trigger conditions are met. A-N5 (Delegation Boundaries) governs scope, duration, and revocation of delegated authority. IPS Framework escalation paths are pre-defined and tested.

### H8 — Override Protocol Invariance

**Statement:** The HAN retains override authority over all AI-performed institutional processes at all times. Emergency suspension capability must be present at all levels. Override mechanisms must be tested by non-technical principals. Override authority extends to IPS-related suspensions.

**Violation Condition:** Override mechanisms untested; invocation requiring system-level access; override invoked by technical personnel only.

**Enforcement:** A-N3 (Override Protocols) governs conditions and mechanisms for human override. ADTEP Escalation Flag forces HAN review. A-N3 is tested by non-technical principal per governance cycle. IPS Framework Level 3+ detection triggers HAN override authority.

---

## LAYER 3: AUTHORITY ACCOUNTABILITY (H9–H12)

### H9 — Performance Review Invariance

**Statement:** The HAN is evaluated annually by the Board against defined performance metrics. Evaluation integrates HAN self-evaluation, external audit findings, and Board assessment. Performance metrics include IPS detection and mitigation effectiveness.

**Violation Condition:** No formal performance review; evaluation is ad-hoc or politically motivated.

**Enforcement:** Performance and Accountability Framework defines evaluation metrics, process, and timeline. HAN Scorecard System provides per-role and per-person assessment (9 dimensions \+ composite score). Governor's Reasoning Audit provides independent external validation. IPS detection and mitigation metrics are included.

### H10 — External Review Invariance

**Statement:** The HAN's reasoning—certifications, suspensions, opinions, findings, and IPS responses—is audited annually by an independent external review node. The review node is structurally independent of the HAN, Board, and C-suite.

**Violation Condition:** No external audit; external audit is controlled by the HAN or Board.

**Enforcement:** Governor's Reasoning Audit is conducted by an independent external review node. Audit findings are published in Governor's Public Register. CEN-5 (Independence) requires structural separation. IPS responses are included in the audit scope.

### H11 — Succession and Continuity Invariance

**Statement:** Succession of accountability must be defined for all critical human authority roles. Institutional function must persist through HAN unavailability, vacancy, or incapacity. IPS governance must continue through succession.

**Violation Condition:** No defined succession; institution ceases to function on HAN unavailability; IPS governance fails during succession.

**Enforcement:** Deputy Governor and Third-Tier Succession ensure continuity. MVOS (Minimum Viable Operations State) defines HAN-only operations when AI workforce is unavailable. Reduced Operations State defined in Continuity and Succession Charter. IPS governance responsibilities are included in succession planning.

### H12 — Removal and Due Process Invariance

**Statement:** The HAN may be removed only by Board supermajority vote, after independent investigation and due process. Removal is not arbitrary or politically motivated. IPS-related failures are grounds for removal if they constitute material negligence.

**Violation Condition:** HAN removed by simple majority; removal without investigation; removal for political reasons.

**Enforcement:** Removal requires supermajority vote (two-thirds or more). Independent investigation by external review node precedes removal. Grounds for removal are defined (material negligence, misrepresentation, constitutional violation, incapacity, conflict of interest, IPS complicity). IPS complicity is explicitly included as grounds for removal.

---

## HAN SCORECARD SYSTEM

The HAN Scorecard System provides per-role and per-person assessment across 9 dimensions, producing a composite score.

| Dimension | Weight | Assessment Criteria |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **Decision Quality** | 30% | Decisions made, appeal rate, quality assessment, confidence score |
| **Response Time** | 15% | Average decision latency, on-time rate, bottleneck assessment |
| **Escalation Accuracy** | 20% | Escalations handled, correct escalation decision rate, over/under-escalation rate |
| **Stakeholder Confidence** | 15% | CIO trust, CFO trust, COO trust, Board confidence, composite |
| **Governance Debt Reduction** | 20% | Debt introduced, debt cleared, net debt trend, projection |
| **Detectability** | — | Drift detection rate, false positive rate, false negative rate |
| **Certification Accuracy** | — | Certification accuracy, certification turnaround time |
| **Observability Integrity** | — | Observability coverage, meta-observability integrity |
| **External Legibility** | — | External audit rating, regulatory satisfaction, contestation rate |
| **IPS Detection & Mitigation** | — | IPS detection rate, IPS response time, capture point closure rate, IPS severity levels maintained |

**Composite Score:** Weighted sum of all dimensions. Target: 8.5+ / 10.0

# HAN — HUMAN AUTHORITY NODE

**Status:** Built — AIGIS Alpha  
**Type:** Human Authority Instrument  
**Parent Stack:** HOF (Human Operating Framework)

---

## PREAMBLE

The Human Authority Node (HAN) is the apex constitutional authority for all AI governance within the AIGIS stack. HAN is a single, named human with 5 irreducible properties. No committee, collective, or distributed consensus mechanism may substitute for a single accountability anchor.

HAN is the operational instantiation of HOF's Authority Establishment layer (H1–H4). HAN holds the authority defined in HOF and is governed by HIS-12 (HAN Invariants System). HAN is the human accountability anchor required by ICC-10 I1 (Accountability Invariance) and the primary enforcer of I10 (Anti-Shadow Invariance).

**The central insight:** Authority without a single named human is not accountability. Committees diffuse responsibility. Distributed consensus obscures liability. HAN is the constitutional answer to the question: *"Who is accountable?"*

---

## SECTION 1: THE FIVE IRREDUCIBLE PROPERTIES

HAN is defined by five irreducible properties. These are non-negotiable. Any person holding HAN authority must satisfy all five. If any property is absent, the person is not HAN—they are a speed bump.

| Property | Definition | Why It Matters | Enforcement |
| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **1\. Board Reporting** | HAN reports directly to the CEO or Board Governance Committee, not through CIO, CFO, or COO | Operational pressure cannot override governance authority. HAN cannot be silenced by the C-suite. | Board Governance Cycle Integration; HAN has direct board access; HAN may escalate to Board without operational filter. |
| **2\. Authorization Authority** | HAN can affirmatively authorize AND prohibit Tier 3+ deployments. Not just veto—active authorization. | HAN can say YES and NO. If HAN can only say NO, they are a speed bump, not a gatekeeper. | A-N1 (Trigger Rights) requires HAN authorization for Tier 3+ deployments. CEF Stage 3 (Mandate Close) requires HAN confirmation. |
| **3\. Cross-Functional Mandate** | HAN can direct CIO, CFO, COO, and CXOs on governance matters, including IPS detection and mitigation. They cannot override HAN's governance decisions. | HAN's authority is not advisory. CXOs cannot ignore HAN's governance directives. | HAN's Cross-Functional Mandate is defined in the Constitutional Charter. Control Node Authority Matrix maps AICA-5 nodes to decision-makers—HAN is the apex for cognitive integrity and IPS governance. |
| **4\. Budget Independence** | Governance budget is locked in the Board-approved charter. Cannot be reduced without Board vote. | CFO cannot starve governance. Budget cannot be cut when revenue pressure hits. | Budget Charter protects HAN budget from unilateral CFO reallocation. Budget Deficit State triggers Board review. |
| **5\. No Competing Operational Authority** | HAN's only function is governance. HAN does not hold operational responsibility for IT, Finance, Operations, or business execution. HAN may not participate in or enable informal power structures. | No conflict of interest between governance authority and operational metrics. HAN is not measured on revenue, cost, or efficiency. | HAN's job description explicitly limits scope to governance. HAN's performance metrics are governance-focused, not operational. IPS Framework monitors HAN for IPS participation. |

---

## SECTION 2: HAN AUTHORITIES (SPECIFIC POWERS)

### 2.1 Authorization Authority

| Authority | Scope | Limitations |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **Certification** | HAN certifies all new AI deployments, capability expansions, and tier upgrades before CEO authorization. No deployment proceeds without HAN certification or formal Executive Override. | HAN may not certify deployments that violate ICC-10 invariants (I1–I10). HAN may not delegate certification authority. |
| **Suspension** | HAN may issue Binding Suspension for any AI deployment that violates cognitive integrity standards, drifts outside authorized boundaries, produces non-auditable actions, or shows IPS infiltration. Suspension is binding for 72 hours, subject to Executive Override. | HAN may not suspend for operational reasons (COO domain). HAN may not suspend without evidence. |
| **Remediation** | HAN may issue binding Remediation Orders to CXOs requiring correction of cognitive integrity or IPS issues that do not warrant immediate suspension. | HAN may not issue Remediation Orders for operational issues (COO domain). HAN may not issue Remediation Orders without evidence. |
| **Formal Opinion** | HAN may issue Formal Opinions—binding interpretations of constitutional standards on matters of cognitive integrity, meta-observability, constitutional compliance, or IPS governance. | Formal Opinions are binding unless overturned by Board supermajority. HAN may not issue Formal Opinions on operational matters. |
| **Constitutional Review** | HAN reviews all proposed AI deployments against ICC-10 invariants I1–I10. HAN may require amendments to satisfy constitutional requirements. | HAN may not approve deployments that violate I1–I10. HAN may not delegate constitutional review authority. |

### 2.2 Oversight Authority

| Authority | Scope | Limitations |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **Meta-Observability** | HAN may trigger AICA-5 Layer 4 Observability (real-time drift detection, decision lineage auditing) and Layer 5 Meta-Observability (observability integrity checks) without C-suite approval. HAN may trigger O-N6 (Shadow Network Detection) IPS monitoring. | HAN may not trigger observability for operational monitoring (COO domain). HAN may not trigger observability without reasonable suspicion. |
| **Access** | HAN has read-only access to all AI decision logs, training data subsets, reasoning traces, observability dashboards, meta-observability infrastructure, and IPS detection logs. Access is granted by Board resolution, not CIO discretion. | HAN may not access data outside governance scope. HAN may not access personally identifiable information without specific authorization. |
| **External Engagement** | HAN engages with regulators, DFIs, external auditors, and institutional counterparties on cognitive integrity, constitutional compliance, and IPS matters. HAN is the external legibility node for cognitive integrity and IPS governance. | HAN may not engage on operational matters (COO domain). HAN may not disclose confidential information without authorization. |

### 2.3 Escalation Authority

| Authority | Scope | Limitations |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **HAN Trigger Register** | HAN is the escalation endpoint for all triggers defined in the HAN Trigger Register, including IPS detection triggers. HAN must acknowledge and resolve escalations within defined timelines. | HAN may not delegate escalation acknowledgment. HAN may not ignore escalations. |
| **Constitutional Escalation** | HAN may escalate proposed deployments that violate ICC-10 invariants to the Board. HAN may recommend amendments or denial. IPS-related escalations are included. | HAN may not escalate operational disagreements (COO domain). HAN may not escalate without evidence. |
| **Emergency Powers** | In a genuine crisis (as defined in Crisis Protocol), HAN may exercise emergency powers: Emergency Suspension (irreversible, no Executive Override), emergency communication with regulators, emergency disclosure, and emergency IPS containment. | Emergency actions are subject to Board ratification within 48 hours. HAN may not exercise emergency powers for non-emergency situations. |

## SECTION 3: HAN LIMITATIONS (WHAT HAN DOES NOT DO)

| Limitation | Why | Enforcement |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| **No Authority Over Execution Layer (E-N1 to E-N5)** | Task decomposition, pipeline orchestration, concurrency governance, monitoring loops, and exception handling are the exclusive domain of the COO and operational teams. | AICA-5 separation of layers. HAN has no authority over E-Nodes. |
| **No Authority Over Authority Layer (A-N1 to A-N5)** | Trigger rights, binding thresholds, override protocols, escalation paths, and delegation boundaries are the exclusive domain of the CRO and risk/legal teams. | AICA-5 separation of layers. HAN has no authority over A-Nodes (except as defined in Section 2). |
| **No Authority Over Business Operations** | HAN has no authority over business strategy, financial decisions, operational workflows, or personnel decisions outside the scope of cognitive integrity, constitutional compliance, or IPS governance. | HAN's job description explicitly limits scope. HAN's performance metrics are governance-focused, not operational. |

## SECTION 4: HAN ACCOUNTABILITY

### 4.1 Human Accountability Anchor

HAN is the human accountability anchor for all cognitive integrity, meta-observability, constitutional compliance, and IPS governance functions. All accountability chains for AI reasoning and IPS detection terminate at HAN. Per ICC-10 I1, HAN is operationally reachable—possessing demonstrable capacity to review, override, and bear liability.

### 4.2 Liability

HAN bears liability for:

| Area | Liability |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Failure to detect drift** | HAN bears liability for failure to detect drift that produces material institutional harm. |
| **Certification accuracy** | HAN bears liability for certification of AI systems whose reasoning is subsequently found to be epistemically unsound. |
| **Meta-observability integrity** | HAN bears liability for failure to maintain meta-observability integrity standards. |
| **False or negligent suspension** | HAN bears liability for false or negligent suspension findings. |
| **Failure to detect IPS** | HAN bears liability for failure to detect Level 3+ IPS that produces material institutional harm. |
| **Failure to respond to IPS** | HAN bears liability for failure to respond to confirmed IPS within defined timelines. |
| **IPS complicity** | HAN bears liability for participation in or enabling of informal power structures. |

HAN does not bear liability for business outcomes of decisions they certified but did not execute or authorize.

### 4.3 Performance Evaluation

HAN is evaluated annually by the Board under the HAN Scorecard System:

| Dimension | Weight | Target |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| Decision Quality | 30% | ≥ 9/10 |
| Response Time | 15% | ≤ 14 days average |
| Escalation Accuracy | 20% | ≥ 90% |
| Stakeholder Confidence | 15% | ≥ 8/10 |
| Governance Debt Reduction | 20% | Net debt declining |
| Drift Detection Rate | — | ≥ 95% |
| Certification Accuracy | — | ≥ 90% |
| Meta-Observability Integrity | — | 100% |
| External Legibility | — | "Satisfactory" or higher |
| **IPS Detection Rate** | — | ≥ 90% |
| **IPS Response Time** | — | ≤ 24 hours for Level 3+ |
| **Capture Point Closure Rate** | — | ≥ 90% within 30 days |

### 4.4 Removal

HAN may be removed by the Board only upon:

| Ground | Definition |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Material Negligence** | HAN fails to perform duties with reasonable care, resulting in material institutional harm. |
| **Misrepresentation** | HAN misrepresents findings, evidence, or reasoning, causing material institutional harm. |
| **Constitutional Violation** | HAN violates ICC-10 invariants (I1–I10), AICA-5 standards, or the Constitutional Charter. |
| **Incapacity** | HAN is physically or mentally unable to perform duties. |
| **Conflict of Interest** | HAN engages in conduct that creates an unresolvable conflict of interest. |
| **Code of Conduct Violation** | HAN materially violates the Governor's Code of Conduct. |
| **IPS Complicity** | HAN participates in or enables informal power structures. |

**Removal requires:** Supermajority vote of the Board (two-thirds or more), independent investigation by external review node, and due process.

---

## SECTION 5: EXECUTIVE OVERRIDE CLAUSE

HAN's Binding Suspension authority could conflict with CEO's operational authority. The override clause resolves this:

| Step | Action | Responsible | Timeline |
| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
| 1 | HAN triggers suspension, citing specific violations | HAN | Upon detection |
| 2 | Suspension is binding for defined period (72 hours) | HAN | Immediately |
| 3 | CEO may override by issuing formal Executive Override Declaration | CEO | Within 72 hours |
| 4 | Override Declaration acknowledges HAN findings, accepts liability, states business rationale | CEO | — |
| 5 | Upon override, system resumes under CEO's liability | CEO | — |
| 6 | HAN's dissent is escalated to Board for review | HAN | Within 24 hours |

**The Executive Override Declaration must include:**

- Acknowledgment of HAN's suspension findings  
- Statement that CEO accepts full personal and institutional liability  
- Business rationale for overriding  
- Declaration that CEO reviewed HAN's reasoning and disagrees on constitutional, epistemic, or risk grounds  
- Acknowledgment of any IPS implications

**This resolves the accountability paradox:** HAN holds the authority to suspend. CEO holds the authority to override, accepting liability. Board holds both accountable.

---

## SECTION 6: HAN CONTINUITY AND SUCCESSION

### 6.1 Deputy Governor

HAN appoints a Deputy Governor with Board confirmation. The Deputy Governor assumes full HAN authority upon HAN unavailability, vacancy, or incapacity. IPS governance responsibilities are included.

### 6.2 Third-Tier Succession

A Board Committee is designated to assume HAN authority if both HAN and Deputy Governor are unavailable. IPS governance responsibilities are included.

### 6.3 Reduced Operations State

Upon HAN unavailability, vacancy, or incapacity, the organization enters Reduced Operations State:

| Activity | Status |
| :---- | :---- |
| New AI deployments | Suspended |
| Existing deployments | Continue under existing cognitive integrity standards |
| Binding Suspension | Reverts to Board Committee |
| HAN reporting | Suspended |
| IPS escalation | Reverts to Board IPS Committee |

### 6.4 Minimum Viable Operations State (MVOS)

If full AI workforce is unavailable, HAN operates as sole institutional actor. No new material institutional actions may be initiated. Existing commitments maintained under existing authority. IPS detection continues through O-N6.
